| **[Perceive-to-Reason: Decoupling Perception and Reasoning for Fine-Grained Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01191v2)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Fine-grained visual reasoning remains challenging for vision-language models, especially when small but critical visual cues are buried in high-resolution images. Existing approaches rely on repeated cropping or test-time visual search to introduce local evidence, but they typically do not explicitly distinguish perception from reasoning. In this paper, we propose Perceive-to-Reason (P2R), a unified framework that formulates fine-grained visual reasoning as a two-stage process: the model first localizes question-relevant evidence as a Perceiver, and then answers the question as a Reasoner based on the annotated image and cropped regions. To better align training with this decoupled formulation, we further introduce Perception-Reasoning Alternating GRPO (PRA-GRPO), a role-aware reinforcement learning strategy that alternates between perception-focused and reasoning-focused updates using only final-answer supervision. Built on top of Qwen3-VL-Instruct-2B/4B/8B, P2R consistently improves performance across model scales. In particular, P2R-4B achieves 93.2% on V-Star, 81.9% on HR-Bench-4K, and 80.5% on HR-Bench-8K, substantially outperforming its corresponding backbone. Further experiments show that the benefits of P2R extend beyond high-resolution benchmarks to broader multimodal reasoning tasks. These results suggest that explicitly decoupling perception from reasoning provides an effective framework for fine-grained visual reasoning.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code:...</summary><p>Code: https://github.com/ZJU-REAL/Perceive-to-Reason</p></details> |
| **[Wildfire-SSR: Geolocation-Aware Multimodal Fusion for Structure-Level Wildfire Risk Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21563v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Structure-level wildfire vulnerability assessment is critical for reducing losses in wildland-urban interface communities, yet existing methods often fail to combine parcel-scale ignition pathways with community-scale geographic coverage. This study introduces Wildfire-SSR (Satellite and Street-view for wildfire Risk), a geolocation-aware multimodal framework that integrates macro-scale satellite context with micro-scale street-view evidence. A Vision-Language Model extracts wildfire-relevant building and site attributes from pre-event street-view panoramas and converts them into structured semantic text. These features are spatially aligned with satellite imagery through a decoupled cross-attention fusion decoder, while a frozen satellite-pretrained DINOv3 encoder captures overhead visual context. Alternative geolocation embedding strategies are evaluated to anchor text-derived features within each satellite patch. The framework is tested on approximately 37,000 structures affected by the 2025 Eaton and Palisades fires in Los Angeles, using wildfire risk labels derived from relative post-event damage patterns to better represent inherent structural vulnerability. Rotary Positional Embedding provides the strongest spatial grounding, achieving a building localization F1 score of 0.6735 and a harmonic mean F1 score of 0.2476 for five-class pixel-level risk classification, outperforming unimodal baselines and an unanchored multimodal configuration. The model also shows a conservative bias in higher-risk classes, reducing severe-risk underestimation, and completes end-to-end assessment of the study area in 37 minutes on a single GPU. Wildfire-SSR reframes cross-view fusion as localized semantic alignment and provides a scalable tool for mitigation prioritization, resilience planning, and insurance-oriented wildfire risk assessment.</p></details> |  |
| **[[CLS] and [EOS] Tokens are not Enough for CLIP-based Class-Incremental Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21440v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Class-Incremental Learning (CIL) aims to empower models to continuously acquire new knowledge while mitigating catastrophic forgetting. With the advent of vision-language models like CLIP, leveraging their cross-modal alignment capabilities to address CIL challenges has emerged as a dominant paradigm. Existing methods leverage CLIP's global representations (i.e., the visual [CLS] token and the textual [EOS] token) for cross-modal alignment. While achieving effective classification, they overlook the rich patch-level visual features and token-level textual semantics inherent in CLIP. For example, the [CLS] token aggregates local information to capture the general concept of "cat", while the patch-level details retain specific localized features essential for recognition, such as the tail, fur, and face. We propose PACE (PAtch-token Cross-modal Enhancement) to uncover the long-neglected local representations within CLIP. First, based on the global representations like [CLS] token and [EOS] token, we identify the top-k most crucial visual patches and textual tokens. Subsequently, to better utilize the extracted key local features, we design a cross-modal fusion module. This module leverages local textual semantics to refine visual representations, while augmenting textual semantics with local visual features. As the fusion module is shared across all tasks, directly fine-tuning it on new tasks would interfere with the knowledge learned from previous tasks, leading to catastrophic forgetting. To alleviate this, we employ a projection-based update strategy to effectively preserve the critical parameter space of previous tasks during the adaptation to new tasks. Extensive experiments demonstrate that PACE significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods across multiple CIL benchmarks.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to TMM. Code is available at: https://github.com/LAMDA-CL/PACE</p></details> |
| **[ForeSay: Tell the Future in Words, Branch the Details in Noise for Precipitation Nowcasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20815v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Precipitation nowcasting involves two distinct scales of uncertainty: macroscopic storm evolution and fine-scale local convective variations. Current generative ensemble methods entangle both scales within independent random noise draws. This coupling causes two practical limitations: every ensemble member must execute the full sampling trajectory from scratch, so an N-member ensemble costs N times a single forecast; moreover, divergent trajectories spatially misalign heavy-rain echoes across members, causing standard pixel-wise averaging to blur critical high-intensity extremes. We present ForeSay, a generative nowcasting framework that decouples macroscopic evolution prediction from local stochastic detail generation. Given historical radar frames, a vision-language model first predicts a textual scenario of future storm dynamics to condition a Rectified Flow generator. Guided by this shared macroscopic prior, ensemble members share early trunk sampling steps to establish the storm trajectory and branch with fresh noise only in later steps to explore local convective details. Because members then differ mainly in local details, self-anchored support-consensus decoding replaces pixel averaging with discrete support voting to preserve sharp heavy-rain cores. Across six datasets, ForeSay achieves the best CSI at the highest precipitation thresholds, with relative improvements over the strongest baseline on each dataset ranging from over 10% to 84%. Our 8-member ensemble retains the forecasting skill and diversity of eight independently sampled runs while reducing computational cost from 8x to 5.2x that of a single forecast.</p></details> |  |
| **[UNITE: Consolidating Embodied VLM Specialists via Shared-Anchor One-Bit Factorization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20767v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied capabilities such as visual grounding, spatial reasoning, navigation, and driving understanding are often developed in separate vision-language specialists fine-tuned from a common pretrained model.Deploying the specialists separately multiplies storage, whereas merging them into one dense model forces conflicting task updates to share parameters and loses part of each specialist's expertise.We introduce UNITE, a framework that consolidates such specialists into a compact shared-backbone mixture of experts. | **[Example-Based Spatial Guidance for Training-Free Concept Erasure in Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20623v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Text-to-image (T2I) diffusion models often synthesize policy-violating content, necessitating robust safeguards and rigorous evaluation frameworks. | **[Learning Latent Volumetric Dynamics for Multimodal Goal-Conditioned Robot Manipulation of Hair](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.06199v3)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Hair care is an essential daily activity for many, yet it remains inaccessible to individuals with limited mobility and challenging for robot systems due to the fine-grained physical structure and complex dynamics of hair. | **[One-Shot Calibration via Model Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20562v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Model editing aims to correct errors in large, pretrained models by replacing model generations with user-provided responses. To facilitate systematic evaluation, we further introduce FiReIQ-Bench, a benchmark spanning financial receipts and legal contracts. Experiments with two reasoning VLMs show that FiReIQ consistently improves confidence discrimination, selective prediction, and probabilistic accuracy over existing estimators. These gains extend to private financial service data, where FiReIQ improves AUROC by 6.8% and coverage at 95% precision by 10.6% over an existing baseline. Together, these results highlight the potential of FiReIQ to reduce manual review while enabling more reliable document automation.</p></details> | Work in Progress |
| **[Vision-Language-Action Models: A Life-Cycle Survey of Design, Training and Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18372v1)** | 2026-11-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-language-action (VLA) models adapt web-pretrained vision-language models to output robot actions, so that web knowledge carries over to physical control. In under 4 years the field has moved from RT-2, the first VLA, to open models that fine-tune on a single consumer GPU and to proprietary generalists that clean unseen homes. This survey reviews 88 works from December 2022 to October 2026, mainly on manipulation and navigation, plus humanoid control, world-action models and evaluation. We follow a VLA through its life cycle, from model design to training and deployment, along seven axes. Model design covers (a) inputs and memory, what the model observes; (b) architecture, how its pipeline is built; (c) action output, how it produces motor commands; and (d) intermediate predictions, what else it predicts, from reasoning steps to future observations. Training covers (e) pretraining data, the mix of robot, web, human and simulated data, and (f) post-training, adaptation to the target robot. Deployment covers (g) real-time execution, keeping pace with the robot. We analyse 22 models on these axes and report their training data, model size, inference speed and hardware. Three trends emerge: (i) training data has broadened, from about 10,000 hours of teleoperated robot data ($π_0$) to 23,000 community-collected episodes (SmolVLA) and 20,000 hours of egocentric human video (GR00T N1.7); (ii) model sizes have split: most recent models have 3-7 billion parameters, while a new group under one billion runs on consumer GPUs; and (iii) generalist VLAs are emerging that handle both manipulation and navigation, such as UniVLA and Qwen-VLA. We also compare VLAs with world-action models, which predict future observations as well as actions. | **[VLAN: Vision-Language Accessible Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18346v1)** | 2026-11-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Accessible mobility is user-conditioned rather than scene-intrinsic, as walkable paths for one embodiment are inaccessible to another. For each trajectory, VLAN provides embodiment-specific actions and accessibility features for legged and wheeled agents, including humanoid and quadruped robots and people with visual or mobility impairments, while everyday obstacles and accessibility labels are categorized as Passable, Bypass, HighRisk, and Blocked. Unlike goal-only evaluation, our benchmark measures physical safety and alignment with user-specific constraints via goal-reaching, collision-aware, and accessibility-aligned metrics. We benchmark modern vision-language models and find they often fail to consistently adapt obstacle relevance, path selection, and safety criteria across user profiles, suggesting accessibility-aware navigation requires dedicated datasets, tasks, and metrics beyond conventional embodied navigation evaluation. All data and code open-source at https://github.com/InSAI-Lab/VLAN.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to NeurIPS 2026 Evaluations and Datasets Track. 25 pages, 17 tables, 6 figures. Code: https://github.com/InSAI-Lab/VLAN</p></details> |

Existing remedies either require costly multilingual annotations or external translation/reward models, or intervene at inference time in ways that can distort the output language. VEPO first localizes key visual evidence using English queries via mid-layer cross-modal attention, then constructs preference pairs that share the same correct answer but differ in evidence sufficiency (full image vs.\ key-region masked image). Direct preference optimization on these pairs encourages low-resource inputs to prefer answers supported by complete visual evidence. Experiments on multilingual POPE and MME with Chinese, French, and Hindi show consistent hallucination reduction and perception gains, with the largest improvements on the lowest-resource language. Analyses further confirm that VEPO sharpens question-relevant attention and that evidence-guided masking outperforms random masking.</p></details> |  |

EoT-SFT teaches fine-grained observation and faithful reasoning through description, analysis, and supported conclusions. RTPO independently samples responses to scene-matched image pairs with fine-grained visual differences under a shared task. Its rubric-based verification combines Gravity, which checks visual fidelity and description coverage, and Flight, which takes descriptions as premises to assess evidence use and relations between paired judgments. Using 1,008 demonstrations and 500 training pairs for harbor specialization, G&F achieves a mean score of 69.12 across six evaluation sets, exceeding the backbone and supervised initialization by 9.76 and 4.56 points. Accepted for publication in the proceedings of IEEE BIBM 2026. The code is publicly available: https://github.com/Advanced-Vision-and-Learning-Lab/ALD_VLM</p></details> |
| **[Is the Geometry Doing the Work? We ask whether trained MERU, HyCoCLIP, and PHyCLIP models actually use these mechanisms. We audit seven released checkpoints and matched from-scratch interventions. All audited converged checkpoints remain near-Euclidean in the dimensionless radius $u=\sqrt{c}ρ$: the largest observed image-side value is $0.37$ -- well below $u\approx0.84$, where local metric distortion reaches $10\%$. Releasing the curvature floor changes curvature and norms but not this regime, with mixed, generally modest downstream shifts. Trained entailment cones are saturated or nearly saturated, so low violation rates can arise from trivially wide cones rather than learned order. Preregistered semantic traversal detects weak within-branch order but no operative full-hierarchy readout. Shuffle-controlled tests detect no pair-specific radial ordering in released checkpoints, and no positive result is consistent across all three matched ViT-B seeds. This non-detection is sensitivity-bounded: the diagnostic reaches $80\%$ power only at $\approx13.5$ pp of shuffle-controlled excess, while the largest released gap is $\approx5$ pp. We trace this to a low-curvature shortcut: lowering curvature widens entailment cones and suppresses violations without learning order. In the probed trajectories, gradient decomposition identifies entailment as the dominant curvature-lowering pressure during collapse. Yet curvature contracts even when entailment is removed, so the shortcut is not the sole cause. Under our diagnostics, the audited formulations do not demonstrate an operative radial or cone-based hierarchy. We distill the audit into a five-number geometry report for evaluating future hierarchy claims.</p></details> | <details><summary>55 pa...</summary><p>55 pages, 7 figures. Published in Transactions on Machine Learning Research (11/2026). Review: https://openreview.net/forum?id=Rj5ewBz62j</p></details> |

| **[Do More Modalities Always Help? A Geometric Perspective on Missing-Modality Robustness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04792v3)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Missing modality remains a longstanding challenge in multimodal learning. Existing methods typically address this issue through modality recovery or adaptive strategies. However, they overlook models' internal cross-modal dependencies formed during multimodal training, which later impair robustness. This pattern appears across diverse architectures, such as fusion models, CLIP-style two-tower models, and vision-language models. We show that such degradation is closely associated with learned cross-modal dependencies in the principal parameter subspaces. Multimodal training induces structured rotations of these subspaces, particularly in cross-modal interaction layers. These rotations are associated with reduced task-aligned margins and larger task-aware representation harm under missing-modality inputs. We propose Geodesic Unlearning (GU), a lightweight parameter-editing method that leverages Grassmannian subspace geometry for structured subspace correction to improve missing-modality robustness. It rotates the principal input subspace toward a unimodal reference along a geodesic path. We prove that this correction minimizes the distance to the reference within a fixed subspace-distance budget. Experiments across architectures and datasets show that GU improves performance under missing-modality inference while preserving full-modality accuracy, outperforming strong missing-modality robustness baselines. | **[DisasterVLG: Vision-Language Grounding and Evidence-Based Reasoning for Disaster Scene Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12144v2)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>General-purpose vision-language models struggle with crisis imagery due to its inherent noise, ambiguity, and high variability across disaster events - yet timely, accurate scene understanding is critical for humanitarian response. Project page at https://sapeirone.github.io/grace/</p></details> |

Although artificial intelligence has advanced image interpretation, existing medical models often address restricted tasks, while general-purpose proprietary models lack access to private clinical imaging required to achieve medical expertise. | **[Decide Before You Move: Diffusion-Generated Search Reference for Zero-Shot Open-Vocabulary Object Navigation in Semi-Static Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15093v1)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Open-world navigation requires a robot to locate a user-specified target in an indoor scene under open-vocabulary queries. ### Visual Prompt Reasoning
> Visual prompting and reasoning methods that may support planning, navigation, and driving decisions.

| **[Reaching Is Not Finding: Map-Anchored Visual Prompting for the Last Mile of Aerial Vision-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11510v1)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Aerial vision-language navigation (VLN) asks a drone to find an object described in natural language. The description usually locates the object relative to named landmarks, such as streets and buildings. We observe that current navigators often bring the drone close to the target and then stop at the wrong place: they reach the target without finding it. This gap is shared by navigators of very different designs, from imitation learners to a fine-tuned 7B vision-language model. A simple test reveals the cause. When we replace the target object in the instruction with another one next to the same landmarks, no navigator goes to it, and most stop where they did before. Navigators learn to reach the landmarks, not to identify the described object. We therefore propose MAP-VP, a plug-and-play module for the last step. After any navigator stops, MAP-VP draws the landmarks named in the instruction onto the drone's current view, using the landmark outlines that the benchmark provides on a map. A fine-tuned 4B vision-language model then locates the target relative to these landmarks. The training data are generated automatically from the benchmark annotations. Neither drawing the landmarks nor fine-tuning helps much alone, but together they are highly effective. Without retraining any navigator, MAP-VP significantly improves all six navigators we test on CityNav. It roughly doubles the success rate of the two strongest trained navigators, raising HETT from 25.9% to 54.6% and FlightGPT from 24.2% to 48.9%, whereas CityNav's official detector-based last step adds less than two points to HETT.</p></details> |  |
| **[Attribute-Evidence Learning with Multimodal Prompt for Generalized Category Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12975v1)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generalized category discovery (GCD) aims to discover novel categories from unlabeled data by leveraging limited labeled samples from old classes. Specifically, we design an Attribute-Evidence Learning (AEL) mechanism that treats attributes as localized visual evidence rather than global category descriptors. By interacting attribute embeddings with patch-level visual tokens, AEL identifies reliable attribute-related regions and extracts discriminative local evidence, thereby reducing the impact of noisy or irrelevant attribute cues. Building upon the extracted evidence, we further develop a Multimodal Prompt Learning (MPL) mechanism to bridge local attribute semantics and global visual understanding. MPL converts fine-grained textual evidence into visual prompts and injects them into a multimodal prompt transformer, enabling attribute information to enhance category discrimination through contextual reasoning without enforcing direct global alignment between modalities. Extensive experiments on six datasets demonstrate that AptM-GCD consistently achieves SoTA performance across both fine-grained and generic benchmarks. | **[InsightChain: Optimized Chain-of-Insight Analytics for LLM-driven Data Visualization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06438v1)** | 2026-09-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for automated data visualization, yet existing approaches often frame visualization generation as a single-step mapping from user query to figure or code, overlooking the iterative analytical reasoning process of expert analysts. | **[Is Visual Prompting All You Need? | **[FlowInOne:Unifying Multimodal Generation as Image-in, Image-out Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.06757v3)** | 2026-07-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal generation has long been dominated by text-driven pipelines where language dictates vision but cannot reason or create within it. | **[Visual prompt engineering for video models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25537v1)** | 2026-07-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In the age of foundation models, a model is only as good as its prompt. | **[GAP-MLLM: Geometry-Aligned Pre-training for Activating 3D Spatial Perception in Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.16461v2)** | 2026-07-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) demonstrate exceptional semantic reasoning but struggle with 3D spatial perception when restricted to pure RGB inputs. | **[Visual Prompt Discovery via Semantic Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.16250v2)** | 2026-06-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LVLMs encounter significant challenges in image understanding and visual reasoning, leading to critical perception failures. ### World Models for Autonomous Driving
> World models, future prediction, planning priors, and trajectory reasoning for driving and navigation.

| **[Lazy-MOSAIC: Accelerating Skill-Centric Manipulation Planning with Learned Skill-Effect World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21517v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon manipulation tasks require a robot to perform a sequence of physical interactions with its environment, typically combining several different skills. Planning such behaviors efficiently is challenging: given a library of parameterized skills, solutions lie in a vast hybrid space of skill sequences and their parameters, and evaluating each candidate skill's feasibility and effect with a high-fidelity world model such as a physics simulator is computationally expensive. MOSAIC, a recent skill-centric approach, lets the skills guide a non-sequential search toward the regions of this space where they are likely to succeed; however, it pays the full cost of simulation for every parameterized skill it considers. In this work, we introduce Lazy-MOSAIC, a lazy planning framework that instead searches over hypothesized skill outcomes. A cheap learned skill-effect world model predicts, in a fraction of a millisecond, a skill's feasibility and the world state it produces. Lazy-MOSAIC then uses these predictions to focus expensive simulation on promising candidate skills and, when a hypothesis is invalidated, repairs only the affected part of the search, while ensuring that every skill in a returned plan is validated in simulation. We evaluate Lazy-MOSAIC on cluttered-shelf retrieval tasks of increasing difficulty and demonstrate it on a physical robot, showing that it plans substantially faster than baselines with fewer simulator queries, while matching or exceeding their success rate in every setting. Visit skill-mosaic.github.io.</p></details> | Under review |

With quadratic costs and linear constraints, refinement becomes a cheap quadratic program (QP), requiring only one model evaluation and one QP solve per control step. | **[The Sense Paradigm for World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19796v1)** | 2026-11-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Latent world models predict the future in an embedding space rather than in pixels. An encoder trained only to predict collapses and maps every observation to the same point, so existing models add a mechanism against collapse, such as a target network, a variance penalty or a distribution penalty. These mechanisms keep the embedding from collapsing, but they leave its distances without meaning, and the model cannot tell a reversible transition from an irreversible one. We propose Sense, a single cross-entropy loss over the directed distance $d(x\to y)=\sum_i \mathrm{ReLU}(y_i-x_i)$ that replaces these mechanisms. The loss avoids collapse on its own, and since the encoder no longer needs the predictor, the predictor is fitted afterwards to fixed embeddings by plain regression. On four pixel environments, the return distance learned by Sense detects irreversible transitions with an AUC between 0.95 and 1.00, where LeWorldModel stays between 0.39 and 0.59. On TwoRoom, the navigation benchmark of LeWorldModel, planning with Sense reaches the goal in 100% of episodes against 91% for LeWorldModel. As the goal moves farther away, the planning failure rate of every baseline grows, while that of Sense stays near zero in Cup, Cliff and Sokoban and is the lowest of all methods in Pendulum.</p></details> | <details><summary>11 pa...</summary><p>11 pages, 4 figures, 4 tables</p></details> |
| **[JEPA4R: Embodied JEPA World Models with 4D Point Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19771v1)** | 2026-11-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Joint-embedding predictive architecture (JEPA) world models predict future embeddings instead of pixels and plan in latent space. We present JEPA4R, a JEPA world model that observes single-depth-camera point clouds, predicts and plans in their latent space, and acts through a policy head on its frozen latents; we call this 4D point planning. A decoder on the frozen world model shows what it imagines as 4D point clouds, with a mean point error within 1.1 cm over 200 steps. Under the policy-learning protocols of OGBench and CortexBench, JEPA4R leads pixel JEPA models on OGBench Cube-Single, Cube-Double, Cube-Triple and Scene, and ranks first on MetaWorld and Adroit Relocate with 98.4% and 88.0%, above the runner-up, R3M, at 96.0% and 65.3%. Trained only in a digital twin, JEPA4R and its policy heads run zero-shot on the real arm with both a gripper and a dexterous hand.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code:...</summary><p>Code: https://github.com/AIGeeksGroup/JEPA4R, Website: https://aigeeksgroup.github.io/JEPA4R</p></details> |
| **[Predictive but Not Plannable: RC-aux for Latent World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07278v2)** | 2026-11-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A latent world model may achieve accurate short-horizon prediction while still inducing a latent space that is poorly aligned with planning. A key issue is spatiotemporal mismatch: these models are often trained with local predictive supervision, but deployed for long-horizon goal-directed search in latent spaces where Euclidean distance may not reflect what is reachable within a finite action budget. We present the Reachability-Correction auxiliary objective (RC-aux), a lightweight correction for this mismatch in reconstruction-free latent world models. RC-aux keeps the world-model backbone unchanged and adds planning-aligned supervision along two axes. Along the time axis, multi-horizon open-loop prediction trains the model beyond one-step consistency. Along the space axis, budget-conditioned reachability supervision, together with temporal hard negatives, encourages the latent space to distinguish states that are eventually reachable from those reachable within the current planning horizon. At test time, the learned reachability signal can also be used by a reachability-aware planner to favor trajectories that are both goal-directed and attainable under the available budget. We instantiate RC-aux on LeWorldModel and evaluate it under both continuation-training and matched-from-scratch settings. Across goal-conditioned pixel-control tasks and a LIBERO-Goal extension, RC-aux improves LeWM-style planning with modest additional cost. These results suggest that planning with latent world models depends not only on predictive accuracy, but also on whether the learned representation encodes the temporal and geometric structure required by downstream search. The code is available at https://github.com/Guang000/RC-aux.</p></details> | NeurIPS 2026 |
| **[ContactGuard: Pre-Contact Execution Monitoring with Action-Conditioned Latent World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13438v2)** | 2026-11-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Contact-rich manipulation failures are often detected only after the robot has committed to contact. Given the policy's planned action chunk, ContactGuard predicts its short-horizon consequence in latent visual space and aborts if the predicted future latent indicates likely failure. Its latent world model is trained from unlabelled robot trajectories to predict compact multi-view visual embeddings under planned actions, avoiding pixel-level video prediction. A lightweight failure probe is then trained from a small labelled set of pre-contact clips. At deployment, ContactGuard anchors prediction before an imminent contact event, rolls the model forward under the policy's own actions, and verifies the predicted post-contact latent. Across real-world contact-rich manipulation tasks, ContactGuard consistently benefits from action-conditioned latent prediction over current-state and direct-prediction alternatives. Counterfactual action perturbations further show that its failure score responds to the specific action being proposed. | **[Latent Projection for Latent Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26979v2)** | 2026-11-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Latent world models let an agent plan in latent space. The planner searches for an action sequence that minimizes a cost, and that cost is often the $\ell_2$ distance between the predicted terminal latent and the goal latent. The latent, however, is partly noise: the regularizer that prevents collapse during world model training promotes variance in the directions carrying no signal. We show that the same regularizer also supplies the structure to prune that noise in the latent. SIGReg drives the latent marginal to a standard Gaussian at every step, and assuming further that the latent sequence is a Gauss-Markov process, the transition is linear with its covariance pinned by the map itself, $z' \mid z \sim N(Az,\, I - AA^\top)$. Fitting the state dynamics $A$ therefore yields the covariance at no additional cost, and in its eigenbasis each latent direction carries its own uncertainty. Furthermore, constraining $A$ to be a normal matrix fixes that basis across timesteps, so one eigenbasis measures the uncertainty over an arbitrary number of steps and serves as a stable basis to compute the planning cost on. We then restrict the $\ell_2$ cost to the $k$-dimensional subspace the state dynamics carry forward with the least uncertainty. The correction is post hoc: the encoder, the predictor, the rollout, and the planner are all left untouched, and only a single constrained matrix, $A$, is fit. We evaluate across six environments and show large gains on the 2D navigation tasks, TwoRoom and PointMaze, raising success rate over the plain $\ell_2$ cost by up to $+54.7$ points under a harder planning setting. Code is available at https://github.com/yjang43/lp2.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to the CoRL 2026 Workshop on Modeling Uncertainty in Robotic World Models</p></details> |
| **[Successive Convexification in Learned World Models for Safe Offline Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19466v1)** | 2026-11-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>World models learned from offline data allow an agent to plan without further interaction with the environment, but the sampling-based planners used with them handle safety constraints only by scoring or rejecting sampled action sequences. SCP requires smooth dynamics, so we build the networks from smoothed MaxMin activations and spectrally normalised layers, and bound the resulting linearisation error in terms of the Lipschitz bound and the smoothing width. As SCP is a local method, we start it from a sampled plan. On two Safety-Gymnasium navigation tasks with offline data, this planner has fewer violations than MPPI and CEM drawing 128 times as many samples at equal wall-clock time in our main comparison, though not significantly, and earns greater return at longer horizons, and is much safer than SCP without a sampled start. SCP converged more often on Lipschitz-bounded models. Unlike sampling, it also reports at each step whether its plan is a feasible, first-order optimal KKT point of the planning problem in the model, or that no feasible plan was found nearby. Our implementation is publicly available at https://github.com/youngers2006/scwm.</p></details> | <details><summary>28 pa...</summary><p>28 pages, 13 figures. Under review at L4DC 2027</p></details> |

| **[WA-JEPA: Goal-Aware Predictive Feedback Action Refinement for a World-Action Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19183v1)** | 2026-11-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>JEPA-based world models predict action consequences in latent space, but representative planning approaches rely on candidate-action search, requiring repeated evaluation of action sequences and incurring substantial online computational cost. Goal-discrepancy residual feedback (GDRF) uses differences between the latent representations of predicted scenes and the task goal to guide chunk-wise action refinement. Future-scene context feedback (FSCF) preserves fine-grained predicted scene representations to guide subsequent action refinement. Jointly learned from offline demonstrations, this process iteratively updates the same plan before execution, making future predictions directly participate in action generation without candidate-action search. We evaluate short-horizon goal reaching, long-horizon tasks, and full-task execution. The results show that goal-aware predictive feedback supports competitive task performance while maintaining a compact model and low online computational cost. Crucially, the accurate simulation of neighbors enables a minimal communication scheme that triggers coordination only when necessary in highly congested scenarios. | **[Beyond Prediction Gain: LASR as a Metric for Action-Grounded Latents in World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17775v1)** | 2026-11-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>World models that support planning, control, and interpretable reasoning require latent representations that genuinely encode action. We introduce the Latent Action Support Ratio (LASR), a dimensionless metric that measures, under offline evaluation on held-out data, whether a learned latent provides predictive support for action-related targets beyond the current state and non-action alternatives. We further use LASR to train and audit a lightweight residual adapter, obtaining descriptively better point estimates whose uncertainty is assessed with paired comparisons. | **[Prediction or Persistence? What Future-Prediction Scores Miss About World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15082v1)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Pretrained video world models are compared and chosen as components for robot learning largely by future-prediction scores. On 27,000 future-retrieval trials from household egocentric video, a model must pick the true continuation of a clip among eleven candidates from the same video (chance 0.091), ranked zero-shot by its own training criterion. Each model is paired with a persistence control, an identical copy whose predictor is never invoked, so the paired difference isolates the predictor. Score and contribution dissociate within the native-video V-JEPA family alone: the predictor adds +0.291 top-1 for V-JEPA 2.1 but +0.03 to +0.04 for V-JEPA 2 on the identical trials. Across five models the contribution ranges from +0.291 to negative, and it is negative only for the two action-conditioned models, which we evaluate outside their training regime. Persistence alone recovers the encoder ordering expected from pretraining, a sign that the probe tracks real signal. We suggest reporting each future-prediction score together with its persistence baseline. The gap between them, not the score, is what makes the number interpretable: a necessary check costing one forward pass. | **[Generate-then-Rank: Learning Symbolic Goal Interpretation for Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07825v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Goal interpretation is to translate a natural language instruction into a formal symbolic goal that a planner can execute. ### Off-Road Datasets and Benchmarks
> Datasets, benchmarks, and evaluation protocols for off-road driving and unstructured environments.

| **[GOOSELoc: A Multimodal Benchmark for Ground-to-Aerial Localization in Unstructured Off-Road Terrain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16162v1)** | 2026-11-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cross-view pose estimation estimates a ground camera's geographic pose by matching onboard sensor data with geo-referenced overhead imagery. Despite task-specific fine-tuning, the performance degrades substantially in off-road environments. | **[CRAFT-E: An Interpretable and Editable Neuro-Symbolic Framework for Affordance-Grounded Active Object Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.04231v2)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Assistive robots operating in unstructured environments must reason not only about what objects are, but how they can be used, including non-canonical and improvised uses. | **[AGT-CV: An Aerial-Ground Team Cross-View Dataset for Heterogeneous Robot Teams in Unstructured Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06478v2)** | 2026-10-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Heterogeneous air-ground robot teams combine complementary sensing modalities, mobility characteristics, and spatial viewpoints that can significantly enhance perception in complex outdoor environments. ### Scene Captioning for Driving
> Scene captioning, multimodal routing, and language-conditioned scene understanding for driving.

(2) Convergent content addressing: identical content yields identical ciphertext and identifiers, enabling deduplication without plaintext exposure, with identifiers binding authenticated ciphertext so a keyless Drop verifies integrity (blind verifiability). (5) Jets and Drops earn Safebux verifiably, kept honest by a one-signature proof-of-storage challenge under chilling-effect Proof-of-Corruption, a zero-sum economy that is significantly cheaper than Filecoin's proof-of-replication sealing (which is slow and provides no confidentiality). During inference, the agent initiates traversal from top-level visual summaries and iteratively refines its focus, immediately halting the exploration process upon acquiring sufficient knowledge to answer the query. To facilitate training, we first extract hierarchical video captions from CGBench, a video corpus with grounding annotations, and guide GPT-5 to generate 33K high-quality chain-of-thought-with-tool trajectories. The LongVideo-R1 agent is fine-tuned upon the Qwen-3-8B model through a two-stage paradigm: supervised fine-tuning (SFT) followed by reinforcement learning (RL), where RL employs a specifically designed reward function to maximize selective and efficient clip navigation. Experiments on multiple long video benchmarks validate the effectiveness of name, which enjoys superior tradeoff between QA accuracy and efficiency. All curated data and source code are provided in the supplementary material and will be made publicly available. Code and data are available at: https://github.com/qiujihao19/LongVideo-R1</p></details> | <details><summary>17 pa...</summary><p>17 pages, 9 figures, 8 tables, accepted to CVPR 2026</p></details> |

| **[VideoAtlas: Navigating Long-Form Video in Logarithmic Compute](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.17948v1)** | 2026-03-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Extending language models to video introduces two challenges: representation, where existing methods rely on lossy approximations, and long-context, where caption- or agent-based pipelines collapse video into text and lose visual fidelity. We demonstrate three key findings: (1)~logarithmic compute growth with video duration, further amplified by a 30-60\% multimodal cache hit rate arising from the grid's structural reuse. | **[Context-Nav: Context-Driven Exploration and Viewpoint-Aware 3D Spatial Reasoning for Instance Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.09506v3)** | 2026-03-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Text-goal instance navigation (TGIN) asks an agent to resolve a single, free-form description into actions that reach the correct object instance among same-category distractors. | **[The Perceptual Gap: Why We Need Accessible XAI for Assistive Technologies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02486v1)** | 2026-03-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Artificial intelligence systems are widely used by people with sensory disabilities, like loss of vision or hearing, to help perceive or navigate the world around them. | **[DrivePTS: A Progressive Learning Framework with Textual and Structural Enhancement for Driving Scene Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.22549v1)** | 2026-02-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Synthesis of diverse driving scenes serves as a crucial data augmentation technique for validating the robustness and generalizability of autonomous driving systems. Additionally, these methods suffer from insufficient details in both semantic and structural aspects. | **[RangeSAM: On the Potential of Visual Foundation Models for Range-View represented LiDAR segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15886v4)** | 2026-02-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Point cloud segmentation is central to autonomous driving and 3D scene understanding. | **[Five Years of SciCap: What We Learned and Future Directions for Scientific Figure Captioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.21789v2)** | 2026-01-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Between 2021 and 2025, the SciCap project grew from a small seed-funded idea at The Pennsylvania State University (Penn State) into one of the central efforts shaping the scientific figure-captioning landscape. Supported by a Penn State seed grant, Adobe, and the Alfred P. Sloan Foundation, what began as our attempt to test whether domain-specific training, which was successful in text models like SciBERT, could also work for figure captions expanded into a multi-institution collaboration. Over these five years, we curated, released, and continually updated a large collection of figure-caption pairs from arXiv papers, conducted extensive automatic and human evaluations on both generated and author-written captions, navigated the rapid rise of large language models (LLMs), launched annual challenges, and built interactive systems that help scientists write better captions. We then outline five major unsolved challenges and propose directions for the next phase of research in scientific figure captioning.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to the 5th Annual AAAI Workshop on AI to Accelerate Science and Engineering (AI2ASE 2026). SciCap Website: http://scicap.ai/</p></details> |
